People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. And I kept thinking, you know, maybe I’m just tired, everybody gets tired, especially with work and customers and schedules—retail can really wear you down after a while—but then I thought, no, I probably shouldn’t ignore this. So… that’s why I’m here.

is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even after a full night in bed, I feel worn out by small things like opening the store, answering a few staff questions, or carrying groceries in from the car.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying little mistakes at work, like a display sign being crooked or a trainee looking confused, and I start telling myself I’m a useless supervisor.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: Decisions that used to be automatic, like choosing which endcap to reset first or what blouse to wear, can leave me standing there foggy and second-guessing myself.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: What finally scared me into making this appointment is that I’ve been having these thoughts like, 'If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier,' even though I don’t want to hurt myself.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: Before this slump hit, I had a stretch where I felt almost too sure of myself and then would snap at people over tiny comments, especially when my district manager questioned my layouts.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: For a while I had this burst of energy where I couldn’t sit still after work and kept sketching store ideas, reorganizing shelves at home, and signing up for extra training I didn’t really need.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: During that energized spell, I was convinced I could fix the whole store’s image myself and that the district manager just couldn’t see how brilliant my ideas were.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: When I was in that keyed-up mode, I’d start one planogram and then get pulled away by a customer’s scarf color, a song on the speaker, or a new idea popping into my head.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I was emailing coworkers after hours, proposing new cultural promotion displays, offering to mentor extra staff, and filling notebook pages with plans faster than I could actually carry them out.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still getting to work and taking care of myself, but I’ve started avoiding extra conversations with staff and putting off simple store decisions because I don’t trust my judgment.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on for about two months, since the trouble with the new district manager really started.\n- key: with this heavy, gray kind of feeling, like the color’s already drained out of the day before I even get dressed for the store. And then I start thinking about what display needs changing or who’s opening the register, which is silly because that’s just normal work stuff, but the feeling is already there. I’ll set out my paints at the kitchen table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to pottery class because I used to really love that—there’s something about the smell of the clay, it reminds me of this little studio my friend dragged me to years ago after a shift at the store…\n\nBut lately, when I actually sit down with the materials, I just kind of stare at them. Like I know what I’m supposed to feel, that little spark or pull to start something, but it’s just not there the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Before this started two months ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt this same kind of depression for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly before two months ago, I guess. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… a bit. It’s not like I can’t follow a conversation, but I get foggy with things that used to be automatic. Like at work, deciding which endcap to reset first—I’ll just stand there staring at the displays and second-guessing myself.

And even at home, something silly like picking what blouse to wear can turn into this whole ordeal. I’ll think, “Well, the blue one, no maybe the white one,” and then I’m just standing in the closet. I used to be pretty quick with those things. Now it feels like my mind gets stuck in neutral.

Like I know what I’m supposed to feel, that little spark or pull to start something, but it’s just not there the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Before this started two months ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt this same kind of depression for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly before two months ago, I guess. I’m not sure. It doesn’t stand out to me that way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so? I mean, I still eat. Breakfast is kind of hit or miss, but it’s always been that way—my mother used to say coffee wasn’t a meal, and then she’d hand me toast anyway.

It’s been there a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two months, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, I still try. I’ll set out my paints at the kitchen table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to pottery class because I used to really love that—there’s something about the smell of the clay, it reminds me of this little studio my friend dragged me to years ago after a shift at the store…\n\nBut lately, when I actually sit down with the materials, I just kind of stare at them. Like I know what I’m supposed to feel, that little spark or pull to start something, but it’s just not there the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Before this started two months ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt this same kind of depression for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly before two months ago, I guess. The hard part is, when I look back, this low, flat feeling has really been hanging around longer than that—maybe closer to six months. I kept thinking it was just a rough patch, or the season, or being tired from the store.\n\nI used to rely on things like my art classes or a morning walk to kind of reset me, you know? Like even if work was annoying, I’d go walk or make something and feel a little more like myself. But for months now, those things haven’t really restored me the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate it out, honestly. Some nights feel normal enough, some don’t, but nothing is jumping out at me as a clear problem.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. Now it feels like my mind gets stuck in neutral.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I still eat. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Kind of, yes… though it’s not the same every day. Some days I notice I’m pacing around the living room, just sort of circling, and picking at my cuticles—my mother used to scold me for that when I was a kid, said my hands looked “nervous,” which always annoyed me.

But then other days, it’s like my body is moving through syrup. Even walking from the break room out to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like it takes more effort than it should. So… I guess yes, but it goes back and forth.

Yes… I think so. I mean, I sleep, I’m in bed the whole night most nights, but I still wake up feeling like I didn’t quite charge up, if that makes sense.

And then I start thinking about what display needs changing or who’s opening the register, which is silly because that’s just normal work stuff, but the feeling is already there. It’s been there a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two months, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, I still try. Sometimes dinner depends on what my husband feels like, and then I end up making the same chicken thing because it’s easy. Some days I notice I’m pacing around the living room, just sort of circling, and picking at my cuticles—my mother used to scold me for that when I was a kid, said my hands looked “nervous,” which always annoyed me.\n\nBut then other days, it’s like my body is moving through syrup. Even walking from the break room out to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like it takes more effort than it should. I mean, I sleep, I’m in bed the whole night most nights, but I still wake up feeling like I didn’t quite charge up, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then little things wear me out more than they should—opening the store, answering a few questions from the staff, even carrying groceries in from the car. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… more than I’d like to admit, I think. It’s not like I’ve done anything terrible, but I keep replaying these tiny mistakes at work. Like if a display sign is crooked, or one of the trainees looks confused after I explained something, I’ll go home and keep thinking, “Well, there you go, you’re a useless supervisor.”

Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. I mean, retail signs get crooked all the time—customers bump into things, kids touch everything, and around the holidays it’s a whole circus. But I’ll still take it personally, like it proves something bad about me. So… yes, I guess I do feel guilty over things that probably don’t deserve that much guilt.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is I’ve been having these thoughts that scare me a little. It’s just… the thought shows up.\n\nAnd I kept thinking, you know, maybe I’m just tired, everybody gets tired, especially with work and customers and schedules—retail can really wear you down after a while—but then I thought, no, I probably shouldn’t ignore this. So… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s been… about two months now, this whole up-and-down thing with my mood and energy. It really seemed to start when the trouble with the new district manager got going.\n\nI mean, retail always has stress—there’s always somebody upset about a return policy or a schedule mix-up—but this was different. Once things with him started getting difficult, that’s when I noticed I wasn’t feeling like myself.\n\nDoctor: If things with your new district manager were resolved, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I want to say yes, that if he was out of the picture I’d snap back, because it really did seem to start when all that trouble with him started. It’s been about two months now of this up-and-down with my mood and energy.\n\nBut then, you know, work problems come and go. I’ve had difficult managers before—one years ago used to rearrange the whole floor plan right before inventory, drove everyone crazy—so part of me wonders if this has just been building. But the timing… it really lines up with the district manager stuff.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two months, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most mornings, yes… I wake up with this heavy, gray kind of feeling, like the color’s already drained out of the day before I even get dressed for the store. And then I start thinking about what display needs changing or who’s opening the register, which is silly because that’s just normal work stuff, but the feeling is already there. It’s been there a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two months, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, I still try. I’ll set out my paints at the kitchen table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to pottery class because I used to really love that—there’s something about the smell of the clay, it reminds me of this little studio my friend dragged me to years ago after a shift at the store…\n\nBut lately, when I actually sit down with the materials, I just kind of stare at them. Like I know what I’m supposed to feel, that little spark or pull to start something, but it’s just not there the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Before this started two months ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt this same kind of depression for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly before two months ago, I guess. The hard part is, when I look back, this low, flat feeling has really been hanging around longer than that—maybe closer to six months. I kept thinking it was just a rough patch, or the season, or being tired from the store.\n\nI used to rely on things like my art classes or a morning walk to kind of reset me, you know? Like even if work was annoying, I’d go walk or make something and feel a little more like myself. But for months now, those things haven’t really restored me the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate it out, honestly. I keep trying to think back—like before all this, before the store got so busy, before my daughter repainted her kitchen, which for some reason is what my mind jumps to—and I don’t know that I can point to a clear two-week stretch.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad times, sure. Everyone has. But “depressed like this” for two weeks in a row before this current thing… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind clearly.\n\nDoctor: If the situation with your district manager improved, do you think your low mood would still be there?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, the district manager situation doesn’t help—when someone’s breathing down your neck over endcaps and schedules, it colors the whole day. I remember years ago we had a manager who used to rearrange the break room chairs like that would improve morale, which always struck me as funny…\n\nBut this feels like it’s there before any of that. Most mornings lately I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like everything’s already dimmed before I even get dressed to go to the store. So even if things got better with him, I don’t think it would just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you been worrying excessively about a number of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I worry about things—work stuff, my daughter, bills, the usual pileup. Though sometimes I start thinking about one thing and then suddenly I’m remembering I forgot to buy dish soap or wondering if I should move those old boxes out of the garage.\n\nBut… over a number of different things, all the time? I’m not sure. It doesn’t stand out to me that way. It’s more like things feel heavy than like I’m constantly worrying, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I toss around a bit, but I don’t know if I’d call it “trouble sleeping.” My husband used to say I could sleep through anything—back when the neighbor’s dog would bark at 2 a.m., which drove everyone else on the block crazy.\n\nLately… I guess I haven’t really kept track. Some nights feel normal enough, some don’t, but nothing is jumping out at me as a clear problem.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not like I can’t follow a conversation, but I get foggy with things that used to be automatic. Like at work, deciding which endcap to reset first—I’ll just stand there staring at the displays and second-guessing myself.\n\nAnd even at home, something silly like picking what blouse to wear can turn into this whole ordeal. I’ll think, “Well, the blue one, no maybe the white one,” and then I’m just standing in the closet. I used to be pretty quick with those things. Now it feels like my mind gets stuck in neutral.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I still eat. Breakfast is kind of hit or miss, but it’s always been that way—my mother used to say coffee wasn’t a meal, and then she’d hand me toast anyway.\n\nLately I haven’t really noticed, like… I’m not suddenly eating everything in the pantry or forgetting meals on purpose. Sometimes dinner depends on what my husband feels like, and then I end up making the same chicken thing because it’s easy. It’s interfering a little, I’d say—not like I’ve stopped going to work or stopped taking care of things at home. I’m still showing up, still getting dressed, eating, keeping the basics going.

But at work, I’ve noticed I sort of… pull back. Like if one of the staff starts chatting, I’ll keep it short, even if normally I’d ask about their weekend or their kids or whatever. One of them has a little boy who’s obsessed with dinosaurs, and she used to tell me these funny stories—anyway, lately I just don’t have it in me.

Like even if work was annoying, I’d go walk or make something and feel a little more like myself. But for months now, those things haven’t really restored me the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate it out, honestly. I keep trying to think back—like before all this, before the store got so busy, before my daughter repainted her kitchen, which for some reason is what my mind jumps to—and I don’t know that I can point to a clear two-week stretch.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad times, sure. Everyone has. But “depressed like this” for two weeks in a row before this current thing… I don’t know. I remember years ago we had a manager who used to rearrange the break room chairs like that would improve morale, which always struck me as funny…\n\nBut this feels like it’s there before any of that. Most mornings lately I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like everything’s already dimmed before I even get dressed to go to the store. So even if things got better with him, I don’t think it would just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you been worrying excessively about a number of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I worry about things—work stuff, my daughter, bills, the usual pileup. Though sometimes I start thinking about one thing and then suddenly I’m remembering I forgot to buy dish soap or wondering if I should move those old boxes out of the garage.\n\nBut… over a number of different things, all the time? I’m not sure. It doesn’t stand out to me that way. It’s more like things feel heavy than like I’m constantly worrying, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I toss around a bit, but I don’t know if I’d call it “trouble sleeping.” My husband used to say I could sleep through anything—back when the neighbor’s dog would bark at 2 a.m., which drove everyone else on the block crazy.\n\nLately… I guess I haven’t really kept track. Some nights feel normal enough, some don’t, but nothing is jumping out at me as a clear problem.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I’ll think, “Well, the blue one, no maybe the white one,” and then I’m just standing in the closet. I used to be pretty quick with those things. Now it feels like my mind gets stuck in neutral.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I still eat. Breakfast is kind of hit or miss, but it’s always been that way—my mother used to say coffee wasn’t a meal, and then she’d hand me toast anyway.\n\nLately I haven’t really noticed, like… I’m not suddenly eating everything in the pantry or forgetting meals on purpose. I’ll set out my paints at the kitchen table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to pottery class because I used to really love that—there’s something about the smell of the clay, it reminds me of this little studio my friend dragged me to years ago after a shift at the store…\n\nBut lately, when I actually sit down with the materials, I just kind of stare at them. Like I know what I’m supposed to feel, that little spark or pull to start something, but it’s just not there the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Before this started two months ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt this same kind of depression for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly before two months ago, I guess. The hard part is, when I look back, this low, flat feeling has really been hanging around longer than that—maybe closer to six months. I kept thinking it was just a rough patch, or the season, or being tired from the store.\n\nI used to rely on things like my art classes or a morning walk to kind of reset me, you know? Like even if work was annoying, I’d go walk or make something and feel a little more like myself. But for months now, those things haven’t really restored me the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate it out, honestly. I keep trying to think back—like before all this, before the store got so busy, before my daughter repainted her kitchen, which for some reason is what my mind jumps to—and I don’t know that I can point to a clear two-week stretch.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad times, sure. Everyone has. But “depressed like this” for two weeks in a row before this current thing… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind clearly.\n\nDoctor: If the situation with your district manager improved, do you think your low mood would still be there?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, the district manager situation doesn’t help—when someone’s breathing down your neck over endcaps and schedules, it colors the whole day. I remember years ago we had a manager who used to rearrange the break room chairs like that would improve morale, which always struck me as funny…\n\nBut this feels like it’s there before any of that. Most mornings lately I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like everything’s already dimmed before I even get dressed to go to the store. So even if things got better with him, I don’t think it would just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you been worrying excessively about a number of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I worry about things—work stuff, my daughter, bills, the usual pileup. Though sometimes I start thinking about one thing and then suddenly I’m remembering I forgot to buy dish soap or wondering if I should move those old boxes out of the garage.\n\nBut… over a number of different things, all the time? I’m not sure. It doesn’t stand out to me that way. It’s more like things feel heavy than like I’m constantly worrying, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I toss around a bit, but I don’t know if I’d call it “trouble sleeping.” My husband used to say I could sleep through anything—back when the neighbor’s dog would bark at 2 a.m., which drove everyone else on the block crazy.\n\nLately… I guess I haven’t really kept track. Some nights feel normal enough, some don’t, but nothing is jumping out at me as a clear problem.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not like I can’t follow a conversation, but I get foggy with things that used to be automatic. Like at work, deciding which endcap to reset first—I’ll just stand there staring at the displays and second-guessing myself.\n\nAnd even at home, something silly like picking what blouse to wear can turn into this whole ordeal. I’ll think, “Well, the blue one, no maybe the white one,” and then I’m just standing in the closet. I used to be pretty quick with those things. Now it feels like my mind gets stuck in neutral.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I still eat. Breakfast is kind of hit or miss, but it’s always been that way—my mother used to say coffee wasn’t a meal, and then she’d hand me toast anyway.\n\nLately I haven’t really noticed, like… I’m not suddenly eating everything in the pantry or forgetting meals on purpose. Sometimes dinner depends on what my husband feels like, and then I end up making the same chicken thing because it’s easy. So… no big change that I can point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… though it’s not the same every day. Some days I notice I’m pacing around the living room, just sort of circling, and picking at my cuticles—my mother used to scold me for that when I was a kid, said my hands looked “nervous,” which always annoyed me.\n\nBut then other days, it’s like my body is moving through syrup. Even walking from the break room out to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like it takes more effort than it should. So… I guess yes, but it goes back and forth.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I sleep, I’m in bed the whole night most nights, but I still wake up feeling like I didn’t quite charge up, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then little things wear me out more than they should—opening the store, answering a few questions from the staff, even carrying groceries in from the car. I’ll get the bags inside and just think, “Why does this feel like such a production?” My husband always says not to carry too many bags at once, which is probably sensible, but I’ve always done it that way. It just feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like to admit, I think. It’s not like I’ve done anything terrible, but I keep replaying these tiny mistakes at work. Like if a display sign is crooked, or one of the trainees looks confused after I explained something, I’ll go home and keep thinking, “Well, there you go, you’re a useless supervisor.”\n\nWhich sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. I mean, retail signs get crooked all the time—customers bump into things, kids touch everything, and around the holidays it’s a whole circus. But I’ll still take it personally, like it proves something bad about me. So… yes, I guess I do feel guilty over things that probably don’t deserve that much guilt.\n\nDoctor: How much has this been interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: It’s interfering a little, I’d say—not like I’ve stopped going to work or stopped taking care of things at home. I’m still showing up, still getting dressed, eating, keeping the basics going.\n\nBut at work, I’ve noticed I sort of… pull back. Like if one of the staff starts chatting, I’ll keep it short, even if normally I’d ask about their weekend or their kids or whatever. One of them has a little boy who’s obsessed with dinosaurs, and she used to tell me these funny stories—anyway, lately I just don’t have it in me.\n\nAnd I’m putting off simple decisions at the store, too. Things I should be able to just handle, like where to move a display or whether to adjust someone’s break schedule. I’ll hesitate because I don’t really trust my own judgment right now. So it’s not ruining everything, but it is getting in the way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I toss around a bit, but I don’t know if I’d call it “trouble sleeping.” My husband used to say I could sleep through anything—back when the neighbor’s dog would bark at 2 a.m., which drove everyone else on the block crazy.\n\nLately… I guess I haven’t really kept track. Some nights feel normal enough, some don’t, but nothing is jumping out at me as a clear problem.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not like I can’t follow a conversation, but I get foggy with things that used to be automatic. Like at work, deciding which endcap to reset first—I’ll just stand there staring at the displays and second-guessing myself.\n\nAnd even at home, something silly like picking what blouse to wear can turn into this whole ordeal. I’ll think, “Well, the blue one, no maybe the white one,” and then I’m just standing in the closet. I used to be pretty quick with those things. Now it feels like my mind gets stuck in neutral.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I still eat. Breakfast is kind of hit or miss, but it’s always been that way—my mother used to say coffee wasn’t a meal, and then she’d hand me toast anyway.\n\nLately I haven’t really noticed, like… I’m not suddenly eating everything in the pantry or forgetting meals on purpose. Sometimes dinner depends on what my husband feels like, and then I end up making the same chicken thing because it’s easy. So… no big change that I can point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… though it’s not the same every day. Some days I notice I’m pacing around the living room, just sort of circling, and picking at my cuticles—my mother used to scold me for that when I was a kid, said my hands looked “nervous,” which always annoyed me.\n\nBut then other days, it’s like my body is moving through syrup. Even walking from the break room out to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like it takes more effort than it should. So… I guess yes, but it goes back and forth.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I sleep, I’m in bed the whole night most nights, but I still wake up feeling like I didn’t quite charge up, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then little things wear me out more than they should—opening the store, answering a few questions from the staff, even carrying groceries in from the car. I’ll get the bags inside and just think, “Why does this feel like such a production?” My husband always says not to carry too many bags at once, which is probably sensible, but I’ve always done it that way. It just feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like to admit, I think. It’s not like I’ve done anything terrible, but I keep replaying these tiny mistakes at work. Like if a display sign is crooked, or one of the trainees looks confused after I explained something, I’ll go home and keep thinking, “Well, there you go, you’re a useless supervisor.”\n\nWhich sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. I mean, retail signs get crooked all the time—customers bump into things, kids touch everything, and around the holidays it’s a whole circus. But I’ll still take it personally, like it proves something bad about me. So… yes, I guess I do feel guilty over things that probably don’t deserve that much guilt.\n\nDoctor: How much has this been interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: It’s interfering a little, I’d say—not like I’ve stopped going to work or stopped taking care of things at home. I’m still showing up, still getting dressed, eating, keeping the basics going.\n\nBut at work, I’ve noticed I sort of… pull back. Like if one of the staff starts chatting, I’ll keep it short, even if normally I’d ask about their weekend or their kids or whatever. One of them has a little boy who’s obsessed with dinosaurs, and she used to tell me these funny stories—anyway, lately I just don’t have it in me.\n\nAnd I’m putting off simple decisions at the store, too. Things I should be able to just handle, like where to move a display or whether to adjust someone’s break schedule. I’ll hesitate because I don’t really trust my own judgment right now. So it’s not ruining everything, but it is getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you seem slowed down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anyone’s said it outright… my husband maybe notices when I’m sort of wandering around the living room, pacing and picking at my cuticles. I’m not sure if other people can see it, but I can feel it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
